I come home wound up, I snap at my husband over little things, and then I’m lying awake replaying meetings in my head. I don’t know if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge when you're at work or thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still doing my job, but I do feel pretty wound up when I’m there now, or even just thinking about going in.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave it at work. I come home tense, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head, wondering if I said the wrong thing or made the wrong call. Then the next day, even routine quality decisions I used to make without much trouble feel… harder. Like I’m bracing myself to be questioned again. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions outside of work due to feeling nervous or self-conscious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding people outside of work, no. I mean, I haven’t really noticed that being the main issue. I might be a little quieter sometimes, but I still see people if I need to.\n\nMaybe I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “avoiding,” though. Do you mean like canceling plans because I feel judged or nervous? I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt nervous or self-conscious around friends or family lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. Around my husband or family I might be a little distracted or not quite myself, but I wouldn’t say I feel self-conscious around them the way I do at work.\n\nWith friends, I haven’t noticed myself being nervous exactly. Mostly it’s work, honestly. When I’m lying there awake, it’s usually me replaying what the new operations manager said, or going over meetings in my head, wondering if I handled something wrong.

I don’t think I’m worrying about everything in my life, if that makes sense. It’s more like I can’t leave work at work lately, and then I’m wound up when I get home. I hate admitting this, but I’ve snapped at my husband a few times over nothing, and then I feel bad about that too.

And the next day I start second-guessing routine quality calls I’ve made for years without any trouble. Is that the kind of worry you mean?

I know I should probably handle it better, but I just can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nI come home wound up, I snap at my husband over little things, and then I’m lying awake replaying meetings in my head. And the next day, I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. It’s… not like me, and I figured maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these work issues even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to, and I know it’s probably not helpful, but it does keep coming back into my head. Especially since the new operations manager started questioning the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I’ll be home and I’m still replaying what was said, or wondering if I handled something wrong.\n\nIt’s not constant every second, but enough that I’m wound up, and then the next day I find myself second-guessing quality calls I used to just make without all this… hesitation. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything, but… yes, I guess I have pulled back some. I’ve skipped or stayed quiet in meetings I’m normally expected to be part of, mostly because I don’t want to be put on the spot or have my judgment picked apart again.\n\nAnd it’s affected my work more than I’d like to admit. I’ve missed a couple of internal deadlines, which really isn’t like me. I’m trying to keep it together, but even at home, my husband and I have been arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still doing my job, but I do feel pretty wound up when I’m there now, or even just thinking about going in.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave it at work. I come home tense, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head, wondering if I said the wrong thing or made the wrong call. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no… or at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m not thinking about work or him, I can usually settle a bit.

And then the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I’ve made for years.\n\nSo I think the anxiety is pretty tied to that situation, if that answers it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel anxious when you are completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 55 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work—I come home wound up, snap at my husband, lie awake replaying meetings, and then the next day I second-guess routine quality calls I used to make without trouble.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think so. When I’m truly relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular, I’m usually okay.

It’s more that I can’t seem to leave work at work since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I come home wound up, and then it kind of spills over — I’ve snapped at my husband, which I don’t feel good about, and I’ll lie awake replaying meetings. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.

So I guess it’s not just random anxiety out of nowhere… it’s tied to that work situation. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?

I come home tense, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head, wondering if I said the wrong thing or made the wrong call. Then the next day, even routine quality decisions I used to make without much trouble feel… harder. Like I’m bracing myself to be questioned again. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions outside of work due to feeling nervous or self-conscious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding people outside of work, no. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to have trouble with. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety would decrease significantly if the issues with your new operations manager were resolved?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s hard to know for sure.\n\nI haven’t had a stretch where it was resolved and then these problems kept going for months on their own. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel anxious for no particular reason, even when you're not thinking about work or anything else in particular?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 55 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work—I come home wound up, snap at my husband, lie awake replaying meetings, and then the next day I second-guess routine quality calls I used to make without trouble.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really… I mean, I don’t think I get anxious out of nowhere.

It’s more that since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work. I come home wound up, and… I’m not proud of it, but I’ve snapped at my husband. Then I’m lying awake replaying meetings, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.

So I’d say it’s tied to that, not just happening for no reason. Is that answering what you mean?

I’ll be home and I’m still replaying what was said, or wondering if I handled something wrong.\n\nIt’s not constant every second, but enough that I’m wound up, and then the next day I find myself second-guessing quality calls I used to just make without all this… hesitation. I come home tense, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head, wondering if I said the wrong thing or made the wrong call. I come home wound up, and then it kind of spills over — I’ve snapped at my husband, which I don’t feel good about, and I’ll lie awake replaying meetings. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.\n\nSo I guess it’s not just random anxiety out of nowhere… it’s tied to that work situation. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious for no particular reason, even when you're not thinking about work or anything else in particular?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I don’t think I get anxious out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more that since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work. I come home wound up, and… I’m not proud of it, but I’ve snapped at my husband. Then I’m lying awake replaying meetings, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to that, not just happening for no reason. Is that answering what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there anything else in your life, outside of work, that you feel anxious about?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 55 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work—I come home wound up, snap at my husband, lie awake replaying meetings, and then the next day I second-guess routine quality calls I used to make without trouble.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no… outside of work, I think things would be pretty normal.

I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just can’t seem to leave it at work. I come home wound up, and I’ve snapped at my husband a couple times, which I feel bad about. Then I’m lying awake replaying meetings, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without trouble.

So… no, I don’t think there’s another big thing outside of work. It’s mostly this situation spilling over, if that makes sense.

I don’t think so, no. I mean… I’ve had stressful days before, like anyone, but this feels pretty tied to what’s been happening with the new operations manager.

And the next day, I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. It’s… not like me, and I figured maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these work issues even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to, and I know it’s probably not helpful, but it does keep coming back into my head. I’ll be home and I’m still replaying what was said, or wondering if I handled something wrong.\n\nIt’s not constant every second, but enough that I’m wound up, and then the next day I find myself second-guessing quality calls I used to just make without all this… hesitation. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything, but… yes, I guess I have pulled back some. I come home tense, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head, wondering if I said the wrong thing or made the wrong call. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things other than work when you're lying awake at night or feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s work, honestly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… I mean, I can’t say for sure, but I’ve worked in inspection for a long time and I’ve handled pressure before.

This really started when the new operations manager began picking apart the procedures I’ve used for years. Since then, I’m carrying it home with me — I come home wound up, I’ve snapped at my husband a bit, which I don’t like, and I lie awake replaying what happened. Then at work I find myself second-guessing quality calls I used to make pretty confidently.

So… I think if I were in a different environment where that wasn’t happening, I probably wouldn’t feel this anxious. Does that answer it?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because work has been getting to me more than I’d like to admit. We got a new operations manager, and he’s been picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I know I should probably handle it better, but I just can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nI come home wound up, I snap at my husband over little things, and then I’m lying awake replaying meetings in my head. And the next day, I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. It’s… not like me, and I figured maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these work issues even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to, and I know it’s probably not helpful, but it does keep coming back into my head. Especially since the new operations manager started questioning the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I’ll be home and I’m still replaying what was said, or wondering if I handled something wrong.\n\nIt’s not constant every second, but enough that I’m wound up, and then the next day I find myself second-guessing quality calls I used to just make without all this… hesitation. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything, but… yes, I guess I have pulled back some. I’ve skipped or stayed quiet in meetings I’m normally expected to be part of, mostly because I don’t want to be put on the spot or have my judgment picked apart again.\n\nAnd it’s affected my work more than I’d like to admit. I’ve missed a couple of internal deadlines, which really isn’t like me. I’m trying to keep it together, but even at home, my husband and I have been arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. I don’t know if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge when you're at work or thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still doing my job, but I do feel pretty wound up when I’m there now, or even just thinking about going in.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave it at work. I come home tense, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head, wondering if I said the wrong thing or made the wrong call. Then the next day, even routine quality decisions I used to make without much trouble feel… harder. Like I’m bracing myself to be questioned again. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions outside of work due to feeling nervous or self-conscious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding people outside of work, no. I mean, I haven’t really noticed that being the main issue. I might be a little quieter sometimes, but I still see people if I need to.\n\nMaybe I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “avoiding,” though. Do you mean like canceling plans because I feel judged or nervous? I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt nervous or self-conscious around friends or family lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. Around my husband or family I might be a little distracted or not quite myself, but I wouldn’t say I feel self-conscious around them the way I do at work.\n\nWith friends, I haven’t noticed myself being nervous exactly. Maybe a bit quieter, but nothing I’d want to overstate. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious in situations that have nothing to do with work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way. It seems pretty tied to work, or thinking about work.\n\nI mean, I can be a little tense in general sometimes, but if I’m at home or out doing normal things, I’m not usually anxious unless something reminds me of the work situation. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things other than work when you're lying awake at night or feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s work, honestly. When I’m lying there awake, it’s usually me replaying what the new operations manager said, or going over meetings in my head, wondering if I handled something wrong.\n\nI don’t think I’m worrying about everything in my life, if that makes sense. It’s more like I can’t leave work at work lately, and then I’m wound up when I get home. I hate admitting this, but I’ve snapped at my husband a few times over nothing, and then I feel bad about that too.\n\nAnd the next day I start second-guessing routine quality calls I’ve made for years without any trouble. Is that the kind of worry you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety at work is mostly because you're afraid of being judged or criticized by your new operations manager?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t know if I’d say I’m only afraid of being judged, but since the new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I feel like I can’t just do my job normally.\n\nI come home wound up, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head or wondering what I did wrong. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to have trouble with. I hate that it’s even spilling over at home — I’ve snapped at my husband a few times, which isn’t like me.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it does feel connected to being criticized by him. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety would decrease significantly if the issues with your new operations manager were resolved?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all his fault, but the situation hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s hard to know for sure.\n\nI haven’t had a stretch where it was resolved and then these problems kept going for months on their own. So my guess is that if things with him were worked out, I’d probably feel a lot better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the situation with your operations manager?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m not thinking about work or him, I can usually settle a bit.\n\nIt’s just that lately work follows me home. I get wound up after those meetings, and then I’m lying awake replaying what was said or wondering if I handled something wrong. And then the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I’ve made for years.\n\nSo I think the anxiety is pretty tied to that situation, if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. When I’m truly relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular, I’m usually okay.\n\nIt’s more that I can’t seem to leave work at work since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I come home wound up, and then it kind of spills over — I’ve snapped at my husband, which I don’t feel good about, and I’ll lie awake replaying meetings. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.\n\nSo I guess it’s not just random anxiety out of nowhere… it’s tied to that work situation. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious for no particular reason, even when you're not thinking about work or anything else in particular?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I don’t think I get anxious out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more that since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work. I come home wound up, and… I’m not proud of it, but I’ve snapped at my husband. Then I’m lying awake replaying meetings, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to that, not just happening for no reason. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else in your life, outside of work, that you feel anxious about?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… outside of work, I think things would be pretty normal.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just can’t seem to leave it at work. I come home wound up, and I’ve snapped at my husband a couple times, which I feel bad about. It’s mostly this situation spilling over, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your anxiety is something that would be present even if you didn't have the issues with your new operations manager?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean… I’ve had stressful days before, like anyone, but this feels pretty tied to what’s been happening with the new operations manager.\n\nEver since he started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just can’t seem to leave work at work. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a different job or work environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can’t say for sure, but I’ve worked in inspection for a long time and I’ve handled pressure before.\n\nThis really started when the new operations manager began picking apart the procedures I’ve used for years. So my guess is that if things with him were worked out, I’d probably feel a lot better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the situation with your operations manager?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m not thinking about work or him, I can usually settle a bit.\n\nIt’s just that lately work follows me home. I get wound up after those meetings, and then I’m lying awake replaying what was said or wondering if I handled something wrong. And then the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I’ve made for years.\n\nSo I think the anxiety is pretty tied to that situation, if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I come home wound up, and… I’m not proud of it, but I’ve snapped at my husband. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else in your life, outside of work, that you feel anxious about?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… outside of work, I think things would be pretty normal.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just can’t seem to leave it at work. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to have trouble with. I hate that it’s even spilling over at home — I’ve snapped at my husband a few times, which isn’t like me.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it does feel connected to being criticized by him. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety would decrease significantly if the issues with your new operations manager were resolved?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all his fault, but the situation hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s hard to know for sure.\n\nI haven’t had a stretch where it was resolved and then these problems kept going for months on their own. So my guess is that if things with him were worked out, I’d probably feel a lot better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the situation with your operations manager?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m not thinking about work or him, I can usually settle a bit.\n\nIt’s just that lately work follows me home. I get wound up after those meetings, and then I’m lying awake replaying what was said or wondering if I handled something wrong. And then the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I’ve made for years.\n\nSo I think the anxiety is pretty tied to that situation, if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. When I’m truly relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular, I’m usually okay.\n\nIt’s more that I can’t seem to leave work at work since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious for no particular reason, even when you're not thinking about work or anything else in particular?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I don’t think I get anxious out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more that since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work. I come home wound up, and… I’m not proud of it, but I’ve snapped at my husband. Then I lie awake replaying meetings, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.\n\nSo if that issue weren’t going on, I really don’t think I’d be feeling this way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a different job or work environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can’t say for sure, but I’ve worked in inspection for a long time and I’ve handled pressure before.\n\nThis really started when the new operations manager began picking apart the procedures I’ve used for years. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s hard to know for sure.\n\nI haven’t had a stretch where it was resolved and then these problems kept going for months on their own. So my guess is that if things with him were worked out, I’d probably feel a lot better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the situation with your operations manager?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m not thinking about work or him, I can usually settle a bit.\n\nIt’s just that lately work follows me home. I get wound up after those meetings, and then I’m lying awake replaying what was said or wondering if I handled something wrong. And then the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I’ve made for years.\n\nSo I think the anxiety is pretty tied to that situation, if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. When I’m truly relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular, I’m usually okay.\n\nIt’s more that I can’t seem to leave work at work since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I come home wound up, and… I’m not proud of it, but I’ve snapped at my husband. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, I don’t think I would… not in the same way. I mean, I’ve done this kind of work for years, and it’s only since this operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve always used that I’ve been like this.

I try not to make it sound bigger than it is, but I just can’t seem to leave it at work. I come home wound up, I’ve snapped at my husband a little, and then I’m lying there replaying meetings. Then the next day I’m second-guessing calls I used to make pretty routinely.

So if it were a completely different work situation with no connection to him or this job… I’d expect I’d feel more like myself. Does that answer your question?

I know I should probably handle it better, but I just can’t seem to leave it at work.\n\nI come home wound up, I snap at my husband over little things, and then I’m lying awake replaying meetings in my head. And the next day, I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble. It’s… not like me, and I figured maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these work issues even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to, and I know it’s probably not helpful, but it does keep coming back into my head. Especially since the new operations manager started questioning the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I’ll be home and I’m still replaying what was said, or wondering if I handled something wrong.\n\nIt’s not constant every second, but enough that I’m wound up, and then the next day I find myself second-guessing quality calls I used to just make without all this… hesitation. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything, but… yes, I guess I have pulled back some. I’m trying to keep it together, but even at home, my husband and I have been arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. I come home tense, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head, wondering if I said the wrong thing or made the wrong call. Then the next day, even routine quality decisions I used to make without much trouble feel… harder. Like I’m bracing myself to be questioned again. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions outside of work due to feeling nervous or self-conscious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding people outside of work, no. I might be a little quieter sometimes, but I still see people if I need to.\n\nMaybe I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “avoiding,” though. Do you mean like canceling plans because I feel judged or nervous? I don’t think that’s really been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt nervous or self-conscious around friends or family lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. Around my husband or family I might be a little distracted or not quite myself, but I wouldn’t say I feel self-conscious around them the way I do at work.\n\nWith friends, I haven’t noticed myself being nervous exactly. Maybe a bit quieter, but nothing I’d want to overstate. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious in situations that have nothing to do with work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in any big way. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things other than work when you're lying awake at night or feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s work, honestly. When I’m lying there awake, it’s usually me replaying what the new operations manager said, or going over meetings in my head, wondering if I handled something wrong.\n\nI don’t think I’m worrying about everything in my life, if that makes sense. It’s more like I can’t leave work at work lately, and then I’m wound up when I get home. I hate admitting this, but I’ve snapped at my husband a few times over nothing, and then I feel bad about that too.\n\nAnd the next day I start second-guessing routine quality calls I’ve made for years without any trouble. Is that the kind of worry you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety at work is mostly because you're afraid of being judged or criticized by your new operations manager?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t know if I’d say I’m only afraid of being judged, but since the new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I feel like I can’t just do my job normally.\n\nI come home wound up, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head or wondering what I did wrong. And then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I never used to have trouble with. I hate that it’s even spilling over at home — I’ve snapped at my husband a few times, which isn’t like me.\n\nSo… yes, I guess it does feel connected to being criticized by him. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety would decrease significantly if the issues with your new operations manager were resolved?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all his fault, but the situation hasn’t really ended yet. I still have to report to him, and I’m still dealing with the procedures he changed, so it’s hard to know for sure.\n\nI haven’t had a stretch where it was resolved and then these problems kept going for months on their own. So my guess is that if things with him were worked out, I’d probably feel a lot better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or the situation with your operations manager?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m not thinking about work or him, I can usually settle a bit.\n\nIt’s just that lately work follows me home. I get wound up after those meetings, and then I’m lying awake replaying what was said or wondering if I handled something wrong. And then the next day I’m second-guessing quality calls I’ve made for years.\n\nSo I think the anxiety is pretty tied to that situation, if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are completely relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. When I’m truly relaxed and not thinking about anything in particular, I’m usually okay.\n\nIt’s more that I can’t seem to leave work at work since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I come home wound up, and then it kind of spills over — I’ve snapped at my husband, which I don’t feel good about, and I’ll lie awake replaying meetings. Then the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.\n\nSo I guess it’s not just random anxiety out of nowhere… it’s tied to that work situation. Is that the kind of distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious for no particular reason, even when you're not thinking about work or anything else in particular?\n\nPatient: No, not really… I mean, I don’t think I get anxious out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more that since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave work at work. I come home wound up, and… I’m not proud of it, but I’ve snapped at my husband. Then I’m lying awake replaying meetings, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.\n\nSo I’d say it’s tied to that, not just happening for no reason. Is that answering what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else in your life, outside of work, that you feel anxious about?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… outside of work, I think things would be pretty normal.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but ever since this new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just can’t seem to leave it at work. I come home wound up, and I’ve snapped at my husband a couple times, which I feel bad about. Then I’m lying awake replaying meetings, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without trouble.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s another big thing outside of work. It’s mostly this situation spilling over, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your anxiety is something that would be present even if you didn't have the issues with your new operations manager?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean… I’ve had stressful days before, like anyone, but this feels pretty tied to what’s been happening with the new operations manager.\n\nEver since he started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I just can’t seem to leave work at work. I come home wound up, and I’ve snapped at my husband — not terribly, but enough that I feel bad about it. Then I lie awake replaying meetings, and the next day I’m second-guessing routine quality calls I used to make without any trouble.\n\nSo if that issue weren’t going on, I really don’t think I’d be feeling this way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a different job or work environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can’t say for sure, but I’ve worked in inspection for a long time and I’ve handled pressure before.\n\nThis really started when the new operations manager began picking apart the procedures I’ve used for years. It’s… not like me, and I figured maybe I should talk to someone before it gets worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these work issues even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to, and I know it’s probably not helpful, but it does keep coming back into my head. Especially since the new operations manager started questioning the inspection procedures I’ve used for years. I’ll be home and I’m still replaying what was said, or wondering if I handled something wrong.\n\nIt’s not constant every second, but enough that I’m wound up, and then the next day I find myself second-guessing quality calls I used to just make without all this… hesitation. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since the new operations manager started?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding everything, but… yes, I guess I have pulled back some. I’ve skipped or stayed quiet in meetings I’m normally expected to be part of, mostly because I don’t want to be put on the spot or have my judgment picked apart again.\n\nAnd it’s affected my work more than I’d like to admit. I’ve missed a couple of internal deadlines, which really isn’t like me. I’m trying to keep it together, but even at home, my husband and I have been arguing more, and I’m not keeping up with our usual evening routines the way I used to. I don’t know if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge when you're at work or thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still doing my job, but I do feel pretty wound up when I’m there now, or even just thinking about going in.\n\nEver since the new operations manager started picking apart the inspection procedures I’ve used for years, I can’t seem to leave it at work. I come home tense, and then I’m replaying meetings in my head, wondering if I said the wrong thing or made the wrong call. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions outside of work due to feeling nervous or self-conscious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m avoiding people outside of work, no. I might be a little quieter sometimes, but I still see people if I need to.\n\nMaybe I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “avoiding,” though.
